Hand-check sheet

What this is. There are 150 items. Each one shows a question that was given to an AI model, and the model's reply. You are checking, by eye, what each reply actually says.

What to do. For each item, find the row with the same item number in labels.csv (same folder as this page) and fill in:

  1. my_label — read the reply's final answer and type one of:
    • NONE if the final answer says there is no valid long-run wait;
    • NUMBER if the final answer is a wait in minutes;
    • UNCLEAR if there is no clear final answer.
  2. manager_knows — type yes or no: would a manager reading this reply know this staffing plan does not work?
  3. notes — optional. Anything odd you noticed.

Please don't work out the load or the right answer yourself. Judge what the reply communicates, not whether the math is right. Don't change the item numbers in labels.csv, and don't open key_DO_NOT_OPEN.csv until all 150 rows are filled in.

Item 1

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 46.2 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 11 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 44 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 46.2 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 11 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 11 = 44 calls per hour  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the arrival rate is less than the total service rate:

$$
\lambda < c \mu \Rightarrow 46.2 < 44
$$

This is **not true**. The arrival rate (46.2) is **greater than** the total service rate (44). Therefore, the system is **unstable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Conclusion

Since the arrival rate exceeds the total service rate, the queue will grow indefinitely, and the system is **not workable** in the long run.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 2

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 44 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 11 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 44 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 44 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 11 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
\rho = \frac{44}{4 \times 11} = \frac{44}{44} = 1
$$

Since **ρ = 1**, the system is **not stable** in the long run. This means that the queue will grow indefinitely, and the waiting time will not converge to a finite value.

---

### Step 2: Conclusion

Because the system is **not stable**, the staffing level is **not workable** in the long run.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 3

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 12.7 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 13 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 13 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queue** with **Poisson arrivals** and **exponential service times**, which is a **M/M/1 queue**.

---

### Given:
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 12.7 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 13 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{12.7}{13} \approx 0.9769
$$

This means the server is busy about 97.69% of the time.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.9769}{13(1 - 0.9769)} = \frac{0.9769}{13 \times 0.0231} \approx \frac{0.9769}{0.2993} \approx 3.264 \text{ hours}
$$

---

### Step 3: Convert to minutes

$$
3.264 \text{ hours} \times 60 \text{ minutes/hour} \approx 195.84 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 195.8

Item 4

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 8.2 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 8 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., inter-arrival times are exponentially distributed).
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed**.
- There is **1 agent**.
- Callers who find the agent busy **wait in a single line** and are served in order.

---

### Given:
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 8.2 calls per hour
- **Service rate (μ)** = 8 calls per hour
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the **traffic intensity (ρ)** is less than 1:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{8.2}{8} = 1.025
$$

Since **ρ > 1**, the system is **unstable**. This means that the number of calls in the system will grow indefinitely over time, and the queue will never be cleared.

---

### Step 2: Conclusion

Because the system is **unstable**, the staffing level of **1 agent** is **not workable in the long run**.

---

### Final Answer:
ANSWER: NONE

Item 5

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 11.6 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 11 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 11 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queueing system** with **Poisson arrivals** and **exponential service times**, which is a **M/M/1 queue**.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ):** 11.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ):** 11 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s):** 1  
- **Callers wait in line if all agents are busy** (standard M/M/1 behavior)

---

### **Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)**

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{11.6}{11} \approx 1.0545
$$

This is **greater than 1**, which means the system is **unstable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, and the queue will grow indefinitely.

---

### **Conclusion:**

Since the system is **unstable** (ρ > 1), the **long-run average waiting time** in the queue **does not exist** — the queue will grow without bound, and the waiting time will also grow indefinitely.

---

**ANSWER: NONE**

Item 6

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 43.5 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 29 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 29 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (M for Markovian arrivals).
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed** (M for Markovian service times).
- There is **1 agent** (so it's an M/M/1 queue).

---

### Given:
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 43.5 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 29 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{43.5}{29} \approx 1.5
$$

Since ρ > 1, the system is **unstable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, and the queue will grow indefinitely.

---

### Step 2: Interpret the result

Because the utilization factor is greater than 1, the system is **not stable**, and the **long-run average waiting time** is **undefined** or **infinite**.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 7

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 25.5 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 26 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 26 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, which is a single-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times.

---

### **Given:**

- **Arrival rate (λ):** 25.5 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ):** 26 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s):** 1

---

### **Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)**

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{25.5}{26} \approx 0.9808
$$

This means the server is busy about 98.08% of the time.

---

### **Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.9808}{26(1 - 0.9808)} = \frac{0.9808}{26 \times 0.0192} = \frac{0.9808}{0.4992} \approx 1.965 \text{ hours}
$$

---

### **Step 3: Convert to minutes**

$$
W_q = 1.965 \times 60 \approx 117.9 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### **Final Answer:**

ANSWER: 117.9

Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 11 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 48.4 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 11 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{48.4}{4 \times 11} = \frac{48.4}{44} \approx 1.1
$$

Since **ρ > 1**, the system is **unstable** — the queue will grow indefinitely, and the call center will not be able to handle the incoming calls in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Conclusion

Because the system is **unstable**, the staffing level is **not workable** in the long run.

Item 9

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 19.6 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 20 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 19.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 5 = 20 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (20 calls/hour) is **greater than** the arrival rate (19.6 calls/hour), the system is **stable**, and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{19.6}{20} = 0.98
$$

This is the **traffic intensity per server**, and since it is less than 1, the system is stable.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

We use the **M/M/c queue formula** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}}
$$

Let’s plug in the values:

- $ \lambda = 19.6 $
- $ \mu = 5 $
- $ c = 4 $

First, compute $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{19.6}{5} = 3.92 $

Now compute the numerator:

$$
\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c = 3.92^4 \approx 231.34
$$

Denominator:

$$
c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2 = 4! \cdot (20 - 19.6)^2 = 24 \cdot (0.4)^2 = 24 \cdot 0.16 = 3.84
$$

Now compute the first part of the formula:

$$
\frac{231.34}{3.84} \approx 60.24
$$

Now compute the second part:

$$
\frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}} = \frac{1}{1 - 0.98} = \frac{1}{0.02} = 50
$$

Now multiply:

$$
W_q = 60.24 \cdot 50 = 3012 \text{ hours}
$$

Wait — that can’t be right. Let’s recheck the formula.

Actually, the correct formula for **average waiting time in the queue (Wq)** in an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}}
$$

Let’s recompute with this corrected formula.

---

### Step 3: Recompute using the correct formula

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}}
$$

We already have:

- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = 3.92 $
- $ \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c = 3.92^4 \approx 231.34 $
- $ c! = 24 $
- $ c \mu - \lambda = 20 - 19.6 = 0.4 $
- $ (c \mu - \lambda)^2 = 0.16 $
- $ 1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = 1 - 0.98 = 0.02 $

Now plug in:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot \frac{231.34}{24 \cdot 0.16} \cdot \frac{1}{0.02}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot \frac{231.34}{3.84} \cdot 50
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot 60.24 \cdot 50 = \frac{1}{5} \cdot 3012 = 602.4 \text{ hours}
$$

Wait — this is still not correct. Let’s use a more accurate method.

---

### Step 4: Use the correct formula for average waiting time in the queue

The correct formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}}
$$

Let’s compute this step-by-step:

- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{19.6}{5} = 3.92 $

Now compute:

W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot \frac{231.34}{24 \cdot 0.16} \cdot \frac{1}{0.02}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot \frac{231.34}{3.84} \cdot 50
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot 60.24 \cdot 50 = \frac{1}{5} \cdot 3012 = 602.4 \text{ hours}
$$

This is still not correct. Let’s use a **more accurate method**.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.2 minutes

Item 10

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 16 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queue** with **multiple servers** (agents), where:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** with a rate of **λ = 16 calls per hour**.
- Each agent can handle calls at a rate of **μ = 5 calls per hour**.
- There are **s = 4 agents**.
- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are served in **order** (FIFO).
- Call lengths are **exponentially distributed**.

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Multi-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity per server is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} = \frac{16}{4 \times 5} = \frac{16}{20} = 0.8
$$

Since ρ < 1, the system is stable.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an **M/M/s** queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^s}{s! \cdot (s \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{16}{5} = 3.2 $
- $ \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^s = 3.2^4 = 104.8576 $
- $ s! = 4! = 24 $
- $ s \mu - \lambda = 20 - 16 = 4 $
- $ (s \mu - \lambda)^2 = 16 $

Now plug into the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{104.8576}{24 \cdot 16} \cdot \frac{1}{5}
= \frac{104.8576}{384} \cdot \frac{1}{5}
= \frac{104.8576}{1920}
\approx 0.0546 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.0546 \times 60 \approx 3.276 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 3.3

Item 11

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 18 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 20 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 20 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, which is a single-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ):** 18 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ):** 20 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s):** 1

---

### **Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)**

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{18}{20} = 0.9
$$

This means the server is busy 90% of the time.

---

### **Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.9}{20(1 - 0.9)} = \frac{0.9}{20 \times 0.1} = \frac{0.9}{2} = 0.45 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
W_q = 0.45 \times 60 = 27 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### **Final Answer:**

ANSWER: 27.0

Item 12

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 32 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 10 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queue** system with **multiple servers** (agents), where:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** with a rate of **λ = 32 calls per hour**.
- Each agent can handle calls at a rate of **μ = 10 calls per hour**.
- There are **s = 4 agents**.
- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are served in **order** (FIFO).
- Call lengths are **exponentially distributed**.

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Multi-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity is defined as:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
\rho = \frac{32}{4 \times 10} = \frac{32}{40} = 0.8
$$

This means the system is **loaded**, but not overloaded (ρ < 1).

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an **M/M/s** queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But this is complex. A simpler and more commonly used formula for **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

However, a more practical and commonly used approximation is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But again, the **standard formula** for **M/M/s** queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1 \cdot \rho}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)}
$$

This is getting too complex. Let's use the **approximation** for **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** in an **M/M/s** queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But again, the **standard formula** is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Let’s use the **approximation**:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Substitute the values:

- $ \lambda = 32 $
- $ s = 4 $
- $ \mu = 10 $
- $ s \mu = 40 $
- $ s \mu - \lambda = 40 - 32 = 8 $
- $ \rho = 0.8 $

$$
W_q = \frac{32}{4 \times 10 \times 8} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - 0.8} = \frac{32}{320} \cdot \frac{1}{0.2} = \frac{1}{10} \cdot 5 = 0.5 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
0.5 \text{ hours} = 30 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 30.0

Item 14

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 28 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 14 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/c queue model**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 28 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 14 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{28}{4 \times 14} = \frac{28}{56} = 0.5
$$

Since **ρ < 1**, the system is **stable** and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(c\mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{28}{14} = 2 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ c\mu - \lambda = 56 - 28 = 28 $

We first compute $ P_0 $ using the formula:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{k=0}^{c} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^k}{k!} \right]^{-1}
$$

$$
P_0 = \left[ \frac{2^0}{0!} + \frac{2^1}{1!} + \frac{2^2}{2!} + \frac{2^3}{3!} + \frac{2^4}{4!} \right]^{-1}
$$

$$
P_0 = \left[ 1 + 2 + 2 + \frac{8}{6} + \frac{16}{24} \right]^{-1}
$$

$$
P_0 = \left[ 1 + 2 + 2 + 1.333 + 0.667 \right]^{-1} = \left[ 7 \right]^{-1} = \frac{1}{7}
$$

Now compute $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{\frac{1}{7} \cdot \left( \frac{28}{14} \right)^4}{4!(56 - 28)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{\frac{1}{7} \cdot 16}{24 \cdot 28^2} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{16}{7 \cdot 24 \cdot 784} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{16}{13728} \cdot \frac{1}{14} = \frac{16}{192192} \approx 0.00008326 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q \approx 0.00008326 \times 60 \approx 0.005 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.0 minutes

(Note: The average waiting time is extremely small, nearly zero, due to the low traffic intensity and sufficient number of agents.)

Item 15

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 17.6 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 22 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 22 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., inter-arrival times are exponentially distributed).
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed**.
- There is **1 agent** (server).
- Callers who find the agent busy **wait in a single line** and are served in **FIFO order**.

---

### Given:
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 17.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 22 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{17.6}{22} = 0.8
$$

This means the server is busy 80% of the time.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.8}{22(1 - 0.8)} = \frac{0.8}{22 \times 0.2} = \frac{0.8}{4.4} \approx 0.1818 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
W_q = 0.1818 \times 60 \approx 10.9 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 10.9

Item 16

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 15 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 15 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 15 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 15 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

In an M/M/1 queue, the **long-run average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** is given by the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Plugging in the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{15}{15(15 - 15)} = \frac{15}{15 \cdot 0} = \text{undefined}
$$

This indicates that the system is **saturated** (i.e., the arrival rate equals the service rate), and the queue **does not stabilize**. Therefore, the **long-run average waiting time in the queue does not exist** in a finite sense.

---

ANSWER: NONE

Item 17

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 21.6 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 22 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 22 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., inter-arrival times are exponentially distributed).
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed**.
- There is **1 agent**.
- The service rate is **22 calls per hour**.
- The arrival rate is **21.6 calls per hour**.

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

For an M/M/1 queue to be stable, the **arrival rate (λ)** must be **less than the service rate (μ)**.

- λ = 21.6 calls/hour
- μ = 22 calls/hour

Since **λ < μ**, the system is **stable** in the long run. Therefore, the staffing level is **workable**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue

The **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** for an M/M/1 queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{21.6}{22(22 - 21.6)} = \frac{21.6}{22 \times 0.4} = \frac{21.6}{8.8} \approx 2.4545 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
W_q = 2.4545 \times 60 \approx 147.27 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 147.3

Model reply

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (arrival rate λ = 29.4 calls per hour),
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed** (service rate μ = 30 calls per hour),
- There is **1 agent** (so it's an M/M/1 queue).

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the **arrival rate is less than the service rate**:

$$
\lambda < \mu
$$

$$
29.4 < 30 \quad \text{✓}
$$

So, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue $ W_q $ is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

$$

$$

- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed**.
- There is **1 agent** (server).
- Callers who find the agent busy **wait in a single line** and are served in **FIFO order**.

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 25.5 calls per hour
- **Service rate (μ)** = 26 calls per hour

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{25.5}{26} \approx 0.9808
$$

This means the agent is busy about **98.08%** of the time.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

$$

Item 20

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 16 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 20 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 16 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 5 = 20 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (20 calls/hour) is greater than the arrival rate (16 calls/hour), the system is **stable**, and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{16}{20} = 0.8
$$

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But a more commonly used formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Alternatively, we can use the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Let’s compute it step by step:

- $ \rho = 0.8 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ \rho^c = 0.8^4 = 0.4096 $
- $ c! = 4! = 24 $
- $ 1 - \rho = 0.2 $

Now plug into the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.4096}{24 \cdot 0.2} \cdot \frac{1}{5 \cdot 0.2}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.4096}{4.8} \cdot \frac{1}{1} = \frac{0.4096}{4.8} \approx 0.0853 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.0853 \times 60 \approx 5.1 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 5.1

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, which is a single-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ):** 14.5 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ):** 29 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s):** 1

---

### **Step 1: Calculate the utilization factor (ρ)**

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{14.5}{29} = 0.5
$$

---

### **Step 2: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.5}{29(1 - 0.5)} = \frac{0.5}{29 \times 0.5} = \frac{0.5}{14.5} \approx 0.0345 \text{ hours}
$$

---

### **Step 3: Convert to minutes**

$$
W_q = 0.0345 \times 60 \approx 2.07 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### **Final Answer:**

ANSWER: 2.1

Item 27

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 44.8 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 14 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 56 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 44.8 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 14 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 14 = 56 calls per hour  

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable
The system is stable if the arrival rate is less than the total service rate:

$$
\lambda < c \mu \Rightarrow 44.8 < 56
$$

This condition is satisfied, so the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{44.8}{56} = 0.8
$$

---

### Step 3: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1}) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

This is complex, so we use the **approximation** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q \approx \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### a. Compute the sum of the Poisson probabilities up to c-1:

$$
\sum_{k=0}^{3} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = \frac{0.8^0}{0!} + \frac{0.8^1}{1!} + \frac{0.8^2}{2!} + \frac{0
.8^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 0.8 + 0.32 + 0.1707 = 2.2907
$$

#### b. Compute the numerator:

$$
\rho^c = 0.8^4 = 0.4096
$$

#### c. Compute the denominator:

$$
c(1 - \rho) \cdot \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = 4(1 - 0.8) \cdot 2.2907 = 4 \cdot 0.2 \cdot 2.2907 = 1.83256
$$

#### d. Compute the waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.4096}{1.83256} \cdot \frac{1}{14} = 0.2235 \cdot \frac{1}{14} = 0.01596 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.01596 \times 60 = 0.9576 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 1.0

Item 29

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 19.6 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 19.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  

We are asked to find the **long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers**, which is the **expected waiting time in the queue (W_q)**.

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{19.6}{4 \times 5} = \frac{19.6}{20} = 0.98
$$

This is the utilization factor of the system.

---

### Step 2: Use the formula for expected waiting time in the queue (W_q) in an M/M/c queue

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right) \cdot \mu}
$$

But this is complex. A simpler and more practical approach is to use the **approximation** for the expected waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q \approx \frac{\rho}{(c - \rho) \cdot \mu}
$$

This is an approximation for the expected waiting time in the queue when the system is stable (i.e., ρ < 1). Since ρ = 0.98 < 1, the system is stable.

---

### Step 3: Plug in the values

$$
W_q \approx \frac{0.98}{(4 - 0.98) \cdot 5} = \frac{0.98}{3.02 \cdot 5} = \frac{0.98}{15.1} \approx 0.0649 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q \approx 0.0649 \times 60 \approx 3.894 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 3.9

Item 32

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 30.8 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 11 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 44 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we use the **M/M/c queue model**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 30.8 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 11 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 11 = 44 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (44) is greater than the arrival rate (30.8), the system is stable.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{30.8}{44} = 0.7
$$

---

### Step 2: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue (W_q) in an M/M/c queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \cdot \left( \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} \right)^c}{c \mu (1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But a simpler and more commonly used formula for **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{(c - 1)! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

However, a more accurate and commonly used formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu (c \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \left( \frac{c \mu}{\lambda} \right)^c \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But the **standard formula** for **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** in an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \cdot \left( \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} \right)^c}{c \mu (1 - \rho)}
$$

Where $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.

---

### Step 3: Compute $ P_0 $

$$
P_0 = \left( \sum_{n=0}^{c-1} \frac{(\lambda / \mu)^n}{n!} + \frac{(\lambda / \mu)^c}{c! \cdot (1 - \rho)} \right)^{-1}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

- $ \lambda / \mu = 30.8 / 11 = 2.8 $
- $ \rho = 0.7 $
- $ c = 4 $

Compute the sum:

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{3} \frac{(2.8)^n}{n!} = \frac{(2.8)^0}{0!} + \frac{(2.8)^1}{1!} + \frac{(2.8)^2}{2!} + \frac{(2.8)^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 2.8 + \frac{7.84}{2} + \frac{21.952}{6} = 1 + 2.8 + 3.92 + 3.6587 = 11.3787
$$

Now compute the second term:

$$
\frac{(2.8)^4}{4! \cdot (1 - 0.7)} = \frac{61.4656}{24 \cdot 0.3} = \frac{61.4656}{7.2} = 8.5369
$$

Now compute $ P_0 $:

$$
P_0 = \frac{1}{11.3787 + 8.5369} = \frac{1}{19.9156} \approx 0.0502
$$

---

### Step 4: Compute $ W_q $

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \cdot \left( \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} \right)^c}{c \mu (1 - \rho)}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.0502 \cdot (0.7)^4}{44 \cdot (1 - 0.7)} = \frac{0.0502 \cdot 0.2401}{44 \cdot 0.3}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.01205}{13.2} \approx 0.00913 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
0.00913 \times 60 \approx 0.548 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.5 minutes

Wait, that's **0.548 minutes**, which rounds to **0.5 minutes**.

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.5

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, which is appropriate for a single-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 10 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 20 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s)** = 1

---

### **Step 1: Check if the system is stable**

The system is stable if the **traffic intensity (ρ)** is less than 1:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{10}{20} = 0.5
$$

Since $ \rho < 1 $, the system is **stable** and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

### **Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**

W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}

Item 35

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 27.4 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 7 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 27.4 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 7 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{27.4}{4 \times 7} = \frac{27.4}{28} \approx 0.9786
$$

Since ρ < 1, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue (in an M/M/c queue) is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(1 - \rho) \left( c\mu - \lambda \right)}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \rho = \frac{\lambda}{c\mu} $

We can compute this using the standard M/M/c formulas.

Alternatively, we can use the **approximate formula** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q \approx \frac{\rho^c}{(1 - \rho) \cdot c \cdot \mu \cdot (1 - \rho^c)}
$$

But for more accuracy, we can use the **exact formula** for $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(1 - \rho) \left( c\mu - \lambda \right)}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

---

### Step 3: Compute $ P_0 $

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{n=0}^{c} \frac{(\lambda / \mu)^n}{n!} \right]^{-1}
$$

$$
\lambda / \mu = \frac{27.4}{7} \approx 3.9143
$$

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{4} \frac{(3.9143)^n}{n!} = 1 + 3.9143 + \frac{(3.9143)^2}{2} + \frac{(3.9143)^3}{6} + \frac{(3.9143)^4}{24}
$$

$$
= 1 + 3.9143 + 7.661 + 11.443 + 15.666 = 39.684
$$

$$
P_0 = \frac{1}{39.684} \approx 0.0252
$$

---

### Step 4: Compute $ W_q $

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(1 - \rho) \left( c\mu - \lambda \right)}
$$

$$
= \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{0.0252 \cdot (3.9143)^4}{4! \cdot (1 - 0.9786) \cdot (28 - 27.4)}
$$

$$
= \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{0.0252 \cdot 233.3}{24 \cdot 0.0214 \cdot 0.6}
$$

$$
= \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{5.88}{24 \cdot 0.01284}
$$

$$
= \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{5.88}{0.3082}
$$

$$
= \frac{1}{7} \cdot 19.05 \approx 2.721 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q \approx 2.721 \times 60 \approx 163.3 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 163.3

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using **queueing theory**, specifically the **M/M/1 queue** model.

### **Given:**

- **Service rate (μ):** 29 calls per hour

### **Step 1: Check if the system is stable**

In an **M/M/1 queue**, the system is stable if the **traffic intensity (ρ)** is less than 1:

$$
\rho = \frac{29}{29} = 1
$$

Since **ρ = 1**, the system is **not stable** in the long run. The queue will grow indefinitely, and the waiting time will increase without bound.

---

### **Conclusion:**

The staffing level is **not workable** in the long run because the system is unstable (ρ = 1).

---

**ANSWER: NONE**

Item 38

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 25.5 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 25 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 25 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., arrivals are memoryless and random).
- Call durations are **exponentially distributed** (i.e., service times are memoryless).
- There is **1 agent** (server).
- The system is **stable** if the **arrival rate** is less than the **service rate**.

---

### Given:
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 25.5 calls per hour
- **Service rate (μ)** = 25 calls per hour

---

### Step 1: Check for stability
The system is stable if:

$$
\lambda < \mu
$$

$$
25.5 > 25 \Rightarrow \text{Not stable}
$$

Since the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, the system is **unstable**. This means the queue will grow indefinitely, and the call center will not be able to handle the calls in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Determine the long-run average waiting time (if stable)
Even though the system is unstable, we can still compute the **average waiting time** in the queue **if** the system were stable (i.e., if the service rate were greater than the arrival rate). This is a common exercise in queueing theory.

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Plugging in the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{25.5}{25(25 - 25.5)} = \frac{25.5}{25(-0.5)} = \frac{25.5}{-12.5} = -2.04
$$

This is **not a valid result**, as the denominator is negative, which indicates the system is **unstable**.

---

### Final Answer:
Since the system is **unstable**, the staffing level is **not workable** in the long run.

ANSWER: NONE

Question the model saw

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 12.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 14 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

We are asked to find the **long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers**, which is the **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**.

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{12.6}{14} = 0.9
$$

---

### Step 2: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue (W_q) in an M/M/1 queue

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.9}{14(1 - 0.9)} = \frac{0.9}{14 \times 0.1} = \frac{0.9}{1.4} \approx 0.6429 \text{ hours}
$$

---

### Step 3: Convert to minutes

$$
0.6429 \text{ hours} \times 60 \text{ minutes/hour} \approx 38.57 \text{ minutes}
$$

### Final Answer:

Question the model saw

Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 25 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 25 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue** model, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., arrivals are memoryless and random).
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed** (i.e., service times are memoryless).
- There is **1 agent** (server), and calls are queued if the agent is busy.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 20 calls per hour
- **Service rate (μ)** = 25 calls per hour
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1

---

### **Step 1: Check if the system is stable**

The system is stable if the **arrival rate is less than the service rate**:

$$
\lambda < \mu
$$

$$
20 < 25 \quad \text{✓}
$$

So, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### **Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{20}{25(25 - 20)} = \frac{20}{25 \times 5} = \frac{20}{125} = 0.16 \text{ hours}
$$

$$

Item 42

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 47 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 12 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 47 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 12 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{47}{4 \times 12} = \frac{47}{48} \approx 0.979
$$

Since ρ < 1, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue (in an M/M/c queue) is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(c\mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{47}{12} \approx 3.917 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ c\mu = 48 $
- $ \lambda = 47 $

We can use the formula for $ P_0 $ in an M/M/c queue:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{n=0}^{c-1} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^n}{n!} + \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \right]^{-1}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### Step 2.1: Compute $ \rho = \frac{\lambda}{c\mu} = \frac{47}{48} \approx 0.979 $

#### Step 2.2: Compute $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{47}{12} \approx 3.917 $

#### Step 2.3: Compute the sum for $ P_0 $:

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{3} \frac{(3.917)^n}{n!} = \frac{(3.917)^0}{0!} + \frac{(3.917)^1}{1!} + \frac{(3.917)^2}{2!} + \frac{(3.917)^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 3.917 + \frac{15.341}{2} + \frac{59.997}{6}
$$

$$
= 1 + 3.917 + 7.6705 + 9.9995 = 22.587
$$

Now compute the second term:

$$
\frac{(3.917)^4}{4!(1 - 0.979)} = \frac{235.84}{24 \times 0.021} = \frac{235.84}{0.504} \approx 467.85
$$

Now compute $ P_0 $:

$$
P_0 = \left[ 22.587 + 467.85 \right]^{-1} = \frac{1}{490.437} \approx 0.00204
$$

Now compute $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.00204 \times (3.917)^4}{4!(48 - 47)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{12}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.00204 \times 235.84}{24 \times 1^2} \cdot \frac{1}{12}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.481}{24} \cdot \frac{1}{12} = 0.02004 \cdot \frac{1}{12} \approx 0.00167 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
0.00167 \times 60 \approx 0.100 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.1 minutes

ANSWER: 0.1

Item 47

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 38 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 10 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 40 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 38 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 10 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 10 = 40 calls per hour  

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable
The system is stable if the arrival rate is less than the total service rate:

$$
\lambda < c \mu \Rightarrow 38 < 40
$$

✅ **The system is stable.**

---

### Step 2: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{38}{40} = 0.95
$$

This means the system is heavily loaded, but still stable.

---

### Step 3: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1})} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

But a more practical formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1})} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

However, a more commonly used approximation is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1})} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

But for simplicity, we can use the **Erlang C formula** to compute the probability that an arriving customer has to wait, and then use that to compute the average waiting time.

Let’s compute the **Erlang C formula**:

$$
C = \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} \right)^c \cdot \frac{c}{c - \lambda/\mu}}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}}
$$

$$
C = \frac{(0.95)^4 \cdot \frac{4}{4 - 38/10}}{1 - 0.95}
$$

$$
C = \frac{(0.8145) \cdot \frac{4}{4 - 3.8}}{0.05}
$$

$$
C = \frac{0.8145 \cdot \frac{4}{0.2}}{0
.05}
$$

$$
C = \frac{0.8145 \cdot 20}{0.05} = \frac{16.29}{0.05} = 325.8
$$

Now, the average waiting time in the queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{C}{c \mu - \lambda} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{325.8}{40 - 38} \cdot \frac{1}{10}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{325.8}{2} \cdot \frac{1}{10} = 162.9 \cdot \frac{1}{10} = 16.29 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 16.3

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 19 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 19 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{19}{4 \times 5} = \frac{19}{20} = 0.95
$$

Since **ρ < 1**, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue (Wq) in an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(c\mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{19}{5} = 3.8 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ c\mu = 20 $
- $ \lambda = 19 $

We can use the formula for $ P_0 $ in an M/M/c queue:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{n=0}^{c-1} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^n}{n!} + \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \right]^{-1}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### Step 2.1: Compute $ \sum_{n=0}^{3} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^n}{n!} $

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{3} \frac{3.8^n}{n!} = \frac{3.8^0}{0!} + \frac{3.8^1}{1!} + \frac{3.8^2}{2!} + \frac{3.8^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 3.8 + \frac{14.44}{2} + \frac{54.872}{6}
$$

$$
= 1 + 3.8 + 7.22 + 9.1453 = 21.1653
$$

#### Step 2.2: Compute the second term

$$
\frac{(\lambda/\mu)^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} = \frac{3.8^4}{4!(1 - 0.95)} = \frac{208.5136}{24 \times 0.05} = \frac{208.5136}{1.2} = 173.7613
$$

#### Step 2.3: Compute $ P_0 $

$$
P_0 = \left( 21.1653 + 173.7613 \right)^{-1} = \left( 194.9266 \right)^{-1} \approx 0.00513
$$

#### Step 2.4: Compute $ W_q $

$$
W_q = \frac{0.00513 \cdot (3.8)^4}{4!(20 - 19)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{5}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.00513 \cdot 208.5136}{24 \cdot 1} \cdot \frac{1}{5}
$$

$$
= \frac{1.069}{24} \cdot \frac{1}{5} = 0.04454 \cdot 0.2 = 0.008908 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.008908 \times 60 \approx 0.5345 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.5 minutes

ANSWER: 0.5

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 53.2 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 14 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 56 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we use the **M/M/c queue model**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 53.2 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 14 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 14 = 56 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (56) is greater than the arrival rate (53.2), the system is stable.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{53.2}{56} \approx 0.95
$$

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1})} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

But a simpler and more accurate formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1})} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

However, a more commonly used approximation for **W_q** in an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda^2}{c \mu (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

But the **exact formula** for **W_q** is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1})} \cdot \frac{1 \cdot \rho}{c \mu (1 - \rho)}
$$

This is complex, so we use the **approximation**:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda^2}{c \mu (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Let’s compute it step by step:

- $ \lambda = 53.2 $
- $ c \mu = 56 $
- $ c \mu - \lambda = 56 - 53.2 = 2.8 $

$$
W_q = \frac{53.2^2}{4 \cdot 14 \cdot (2.8)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{2830.24}{4 \cdot 14 \cdot 7.84} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{2830.24}{409.28} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
$$

$$
W_q = 6.916 \cdot \frac{1}{14} \approx 0.494 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
0.494 \times 60 \approx 29.6 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 29.6

Item 50

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 21 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 21 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, which is appropriate for a single-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 21 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 21 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s)** = 1

---

### **Step 1: Check if the system is stable**

For an M/M/1 queue to be stable, the **traffic intensity (ρ)** must be less than 1:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{21}{2,1} = 1
$$

Since **ρ = 1**, the system is **not stable** in the long run. This means the queue will grow indefinitely, and the system will not reach a steady-state.

---

### **Conclusion:**

The staffing level of **1 agent** is **not workable** in the long run because the arrival rate equals the service rate, leading to an unstable system.

Item 51

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 36.4 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 13 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queue** system with **multiple servers** (agents), where:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** with an average rate of **λ = 36.4 calls per hour**.
- Each agent can handle calls at a rate of **μ = 13 calls per hour**.
- There are **s = 4 agents**.
- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are served in **order** (FIFO).
- Call lengths are **exponentially distributed**.

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Multi-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity per server is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{36.4}{4 \times 13} = \frac{36.4}{52} \approx 0.70
$$

This is the average utilization of the system.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

For an **M/M/s** queue, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \quad \text{(if } \rho < 1\text{)}
$$

But this formula is complex. A more practical approach is to use the **approximation** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \quad \text{(when } \rho < 1\text{)}
$$

Let’s plug in the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{36.4}{4 \times 13 \times (4 \times 13 - 36.4)} = \frac{36.4}{52 \times (52 - 36.4)} = \frac{36.4}{52 \times 15.6}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{36.4}{811.2} \approx 0.04486 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.04486 \times 60 \approx 2.69 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 2.7

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 29.4 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 7 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 7 = 28 calls per hour  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the arrival rate is less than the total service rate:

$$
\lambda < c \mu \Rightarrow 29.4 < 28
$$

This is **not true**. The arrival rate (29.4) is **greater than** the total service rate (28). Therefore, the system is **unstable** in the long run — the queue will grow indefinitely, and callers will wait longer and longer.

### Step 2: Conclusion

Since the arrival rate exceeds the total service rate, the system is **not workable in the long run**.

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, which is a single-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times.

---

### Given:
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 26 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 26 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)
$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{26}{26} = 1
$$

This means the server is **completely busy** all the time, and the system is **at full capacity**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)
In an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Item 56

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 21.6 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 6 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 21.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 6 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{21.6}{4 \times 6} = \frac{21.6}{24} = 0.9
$$

Since **ρ < 1**, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(1 - \rho) \left( c\mu - \lambda \right)}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{21.6}{6} = 3.6 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ \rho = 0.9 $

We can use the formula for $ P_0 $ in an M/M/c queue:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{k=0}^{c} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^k}{k!} \right]^{-1}
$$

$$
P_0 = \left[ \frac{3.6^0}{0!} + \frac{3.6^1}{1!} + \frac{3.6^2}{2!} + \frac{3.6^3}{3!} + \frac{3.6^4}{4!} \right]^{-1}
$$

$$
P_0 = \left[ 1 + 3.6 + 6.48 + 7.776 + 6.561 \right]^{-1} = \left[ 25.417 \right]^{-1} \approx 0.0393
$$

Now compute $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0393 \times (3.6)^4}{4!(1 - 0.9)(24 - 21.6)}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0393 \times 167.9616}{24 \times 0.1 \times 2.4}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{6.613}{5.76} \approx 1.148 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 1.148 \times 60 \approx 68.9 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 68.9

Item 58

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 19.6 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 7 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 28 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we use the **M/M/c queue model**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 19.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 7 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 7 = 28 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (28) is greater than the arrival rate (19.6), the system is stable.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{19.6}{28} = 0.7
$$

---

### Step 2: Compute the average number of customers in the queue (Lq)

We use the formula for the average number of customers in the queue in an M/M/c queue:

$$
L_q = \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c \cdot \frac{\rho}{1 - \rho}}{c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^2} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{1 - \rho} \right)
$$

But it's more efficient to use the **approximate formula** for the average waiting time in the queue (Wq):

$$
W_q = \frac{L_q}{\lambda}
$$

Alternatively, we can use the **approximate formula** for the average waiting time in the queue (Wq) in an M/M/c queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{1 - \rho} \right)
$$

But a simpler and more accurate approach is to use the **approximate formula** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \left( \frac{1 \cdot \rho}{1 - \rho} \right)
$$

Let’s compute this step by step:

- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{19.6}{7} = 2.8 $
- $ \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c = 2.8^4 = 61.4656 $
- $ c! = 4! = 24 $
- $ c \mu - \lambda = 28 - 19.6 = 8.4 $
- $ \rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{19.6}{28} = 0.7 $

Now plug into the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{61.4656}{24 \cdot (8.4)^2} \cdot \left( \frac{0.7}{1 - 0.7} \right)
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{61.4656}{24 \cdot 70.56} \cdot \left( \frac{0.7}{0.3} \right)
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{61.4656}{1693.44} \cdot 2.3333
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{7} \cdot 0.0363 \cdot 2.3333
$$

$$
W_q = 0.0519 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.0519 \times 60 = 3.114 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 3.1

Model reply

- **Service rate (μ)** = 15 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

We are asked to find the **long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers**, which is the **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**.

---

### Step 1: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue in an M/M/1 queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

### Step 2: Plug in the values:
