Item 62

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 48 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 12 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 48 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **queueing system** with the following characteristics:

- **Arrival rate (λ)**: 48 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)**: 12 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s)**: 4  
- **Total service rate (s × μ)**: 4 × 12 = 48 calls per hour  

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Poisson arrivals, exponential service times, s servers).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} = \frac{48}{4 \times 12} = \frac{48}{48} = 1
$$

This means the system is **critically loaded** (ρ = 1), and the queue will grow indefinitely unless the arrival rate is less than the service rate. However, in this case, the total service rate equals the arrival rate, so the system is **at capacity**.

---

### Step 2: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

For an **M/M/s** queue, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\rho^s}{s!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But since **ρ = 1**, the formula becomes **undefined** (division by zero). This indicates that the system is **not stable** and the queue will grow indefinitely.

However, in practice, when **ρ = 1**, the system is **at capacity**, and the **average waiting time in the queue** becomes **infinite**.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 63

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 13.7 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 14 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, which is appropriate for a single-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ):** 13.7 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ):** 14 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s):** 1

---

### **Step 1: Check if the system is stable**

The system is stable if the **arrival rate is less than the service rate**:

$$
\lambda < \mu
$$

$$
13.7 < 14 \quad \text{✓}
$$

So, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### **Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{13.7}{14(14 - 13.7)} = \frac{13.7}{14 \times 0.3} = \frac{13.7}{4.2} \approx 3.2619 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
W_q = 3.2619 \times 60 \approx 195.7 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### **Conclusion:**

The staffing level is **workable** in the long run, and the **long-run average waiting time** for a caller in the queue is approximately **195.7 minutes**.

---

**ANSWER: 195.7**

Item 64

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 47 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 12 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queue** with **multiple servers** (agents), where:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** with rate $ \lambda = 47 $ calls per hour.
- Each agent can handle calls at a rate of $ \mu = 12 $ calls per hour.
- There are $ s = 4 $ agents.
- Call lengths are **exponentially distributed**.
- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are answered in order.

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Multi-server queue with Markovian arrival and service times).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity per server is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} = \frac{47}{4 \times 12} = \frac{47}{48} \approx 0.979
$$

This is **less than 1**, so the system is stable.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an **M/M/s** queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^s}{s! \cdot (s \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{47}{12} \approx 3.9167 $
- $ \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^s = (3.9167)^4 \approx 233.3 $
- $ s! = 4! = 24 $
- $ s \mu - \lambda = 48 - 47 = 1 $
- $ (s \mu - \lambda)^2 = 1^2 = 1 $

Now plug into the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{233.3}{24 \cdot 1^2} \cdot \frac{1}{12} = \frac{233.3}{24} \cdot \frac{1}{12}
$$

$$
W_q = 9.72 \cdot \frac{1}{12} \approx 0.81 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
W_q = 0.81 \times 60 \approx 48.6 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 48.6

ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 58.8 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 14 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{58.8}{4 \times 14} = \frac{58.8}{56} = 1.05

- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are served in **order** (FIFO).

Item 72

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 38 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 10 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 38 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 10 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{38}{4 \times 10} = \frac{38}{40} = 0.95
$$

Since **ρ < 1**, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(c\mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{38}{10} = 3.8 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ c\mu = 40 $
- $ \lambda = 38 $

We can use the formula for $ P_0 $ in an M/M/c queue:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{n=0}^{c-1} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^n}{n!} + \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \right]^{-1}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### Step 2.1: Compute the sum for $ P_0 $

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{3} \frac{(3.8)^n}{n!} = \frac{3.8^0}{0!} + \frac{3.8^1}{1!} + \frac{3.8^2}{2!} + \frac{3.8^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 3.8 + \frac{14.44}{2} + \frac{54.872}{6}
= 1 + 3.8 + 7.22 + 9.1453 = 21.1653
$$

Now compute the second term:

$$
\frac{(3.8)^4}{4!(1 - 0.95)} = \frac{208.5136}{24 \times 0.05} = \frac{208.5136}{1.2} = 173.7613
$$

Now compute $ P_0 $:

$$
P_0 = \left( 21.1653 + 173.7613 \right)^{-1} = \left( 194.9266 \right)^{-1} \approx 0.00513
$$

#### Step 2.2: Compute $ W_q $

$$
W_q = \frac{0.00513 \cdot (3.8)^4}{4!(40 - 38)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{10}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.00513 \cdot 208.5136}{24 \cdot 4} \cdot \frac{1}{10}
$$

$$
= \frac{1.069}{96} \cdot \frac{1}{10} = \frac{1.069}{960} \approx 0.0011135 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.0011135 \times 60 \approx 0.0668 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.1

ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/c queue model**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 31.4 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 8 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{31.4}{4 \times 8} = \frac{31.4}{32} \approx 0.98125
$$

Since **ρ < 1**, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(1 - \rho) \mu}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system
- $ \rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} $

We can compute $ P_0 $ using the formula:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{n=0}^{c} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^n}{n!} \right]^{-1}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### Step 2.1: Compute $ \lambda/\mu = 31.4 / 8 = 3.925 $

#### Step 2.2: Compute the sum in the denominator:

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{4} \frac{(3.925)^n}{n!}
$$

- $ n = 0 $: $ \frac{(3.925)^0}{0!} = 1 $
- $ n = 1 $: $ \frac{3.925}{1} = 3.925 $
- $ n = 2 $: $ \frac{(3.925)^2}{2} = \frac{15.4056}{2} = 7.7028 $
- $ n = 3 $: $ \frac{(3.925)^3}{6} = \frac{60.344}{6} \approx 10.0573 $
- $ n = 4 $: $ \frac{(3.925)^4}{24} = \frac{236.84}{24} \approx 9.8683 $

Now sum these:

$$
1 + 3.925 + 7.7028 + 10.0573 + 9.8683 = 32.5534
$$

So:

$$
P_0 = \frac{1}{32.5534} \approx 0.0307
$$

#### Step 2.3: Compute $ W_q $

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0307 \times (3.925)^4}{4!(1 - 0.98125) \times 8}
$$

First, compute $ (3.925)^4 = 236.84 $

Then:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0307 \times 236.84}{24 \times 0.01875 \times 8}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{7.283}{3.75} \approx 1.941 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
1.941 \times 60 \approx 116.5 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 116.5

Item 78

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 20.6 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 21 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 21 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., arrivals are memoryless and random).
- Call durations are **exponentially distributed** (i.e., service times are memoryless).
- There is **1 agent** (server), and calls are served in a **first-come, first-served** manner.
- Callers who find the agent busy **wait in a single line**.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 20.6 calls per hour
- **Service rate (μ)** = 21 calls per hour
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1

---

### **Step 1: Check if the system is stable**

The system is stable if the **arrival rate is less than the service rate**:

$$
\lambda < \mu
$$

$$
20.6 < 21 \quad \text{✓}
$$

So, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### **Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{20.6}{21(21 - 20.6)} = \frac{20.6}{21 \times 0.4} = \frac{20.6}{8.4} \approx 2.452 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
2.452 \times 60 \approx 147.1 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### **Final Answer:**

ANSWER: 147.1

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** with rate $ \lambda = 33.6 $ calls per hour.
- Each agent can handle calls at a rate of $ \mu = 8 $ calls per hour.
- There are $ s = 4 $ agents.
- Call lengths are **exponentially distributed**.
- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are answered in order.

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Multi-server queue with Markovian arrival and service times).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity per server is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} = \frac{33.6}{4 \times 8} = \frac{33.6}{32} = 1.05
$$

Since $ \rho > 1 $, the system is **unstable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, and the queue will grow indefinitely.

---

### Step 2: Interpret the result

Because the system is **unstable** (i.e., the utilization of the servers exceeds 100%), the **long-run average waiting time** in the queue **does not exist** — the queue will grow without bound, and the waiting time will also grow indefinitely.

Item 81

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 19.6 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 20 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 19.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 5 = 20 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (20 calls/hour) is **greater than** the arrival rate (19.6 calls/hour), the system is **stable**, and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{19.6}{20} = 0.98
$$

This is the **traffic intensity per server**, and since it is less than 1, the system is stable.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

We use the **M/M/c queue formula** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}}
$$

Let’s plug in the values:

- $ \lambda = 19.6 $
- $ \mu = 5 $
- $ c = 4 $

First, compute $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{19.6}{5} = 3.92 $

Now compute the numerator:

$$
\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c = 3.92^4 \approx 231.34
$$

Denominator:

$$
c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2 = 4! \cdot (20 - 19.6)^2 = 24 \cdot (0.4)^2 = 24 \cdot 0.16 = 3.84
$$

Now compute the first part of the formula:

$$
\frac{231.34}{3.84} \approx 60.24
$$

Now compute the second part:

$$
\frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}} = \frac{1}{1 - 0.98} = \frac{1}{0.02} = 50
$$

Now multiply:

$$
W_q = 60.24 \cdot 50 = 3012 \text{ hours}
$$

Wait — that can’t be right. Let’s recheck the formula.

Actually, the correct formula for **average waiting time in the queue (Wq)** in an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \cdot (c \mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}}
$$

Let’s recompute with this corrected formula.

We already have:

- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = 3.92 $

- $ (c \mu - \lambda)^2 = 0.16 $
- $ 1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = 1 - 0.98 = 0.02 $

Now plug in:

W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot \frac{231.34}{24 \cdot 0.16} \cdot \frac{1}{0.02}

Item 82

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 36.7 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 9 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 36.7 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 9 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{36.7}{4 \times 9} = \frac{36.7}{36} \approx 1.019
$$

Since ρ > 1, the system is **unstable** — the queue will grow indefinitely, and the call center will not be able to handle the incoming calls in the long run.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 83

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 16 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 20 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **queueing system** with the following characteristics:

- **Arrival rate (λ)**: 16 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)**: 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s)**: 4  
- **Total service rate (s × μ)**: 4 × 5 = 20 calls per hour  

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Poisson arrivals, exponential service times, s servers).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} = \frac{16}{20} = 0.8
$$

This is the **traffic intensity**, which is less than 1, so the system is stable.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average number of callers in the queue (Lq)

We use the formula for the average number of callers in the queue in an M/M/s queue:

$$
L_q = \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^s \cdot \frac{\rho}{1 - \rho}}{s! \cdot \left(1 - \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} \right)} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{1 - \rho} \right)
$$

But it's more efficient to use the **approximate formula** for the average waiting time in the queue (Wq):

$$
W_q = \frac{L_q}{\lambda}
$$

Alternatively, we can use the **approximate formula** for the average waiting time in the queue (Wq) in an M/M/s queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^s}{s! \cdot \left(1 - \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But this is complex. Instead, we can use the **approximate formula** for the average waiting time in the queue (Wq) in an M/M/s queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^s}{s! \cdot \left(1 - \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step:

- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{16}{5} = 3.2 $
- $ \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^s = 3.2^4 = 104.8576 $
- $ s! = 4! = 24 $
- $ 1 - \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} = 1 - 0.8 = 0.2 $

Now plug into the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot \frac{104.8576}{24 \cdot 0.2} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - 0.8}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot \frac{104.8576}{4.8} \cdot \frac{1}{0.2}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{5} \cdot 21.8453 \cdot 5 = 21.8453 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

$$
\boxed{\text{ANSWER: 21.8 minutes}}
$$

Item 84

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 30.8 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 28 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (arrival rate λ = 30.8 calls/hour),
- Call durations are **exponentially distributed** (service rate μ = 28 calls/hour),
- There is **1 agent** (server),
- Callers who find the agent busy wait in a **single line** and are served in **order** (FIFO).

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the **arrival rate is less than the service rate**:

$$
\lambda < \mu
$$

$$
30.8 < 28 \quad \text{→ False}
$$

Since the arrival rate is **greater than** the service rate, the system is **unstable**. This means the queue will grow indefinitely, and the call center will not be able to handle the calls in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Conclusion

Because the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, the staffing level is **not workable** in the long run.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 85

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 11.6 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 11 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 11 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queueing system** with **Poisson arrivals** and **exponential service times**, which is a **M/M/1 queue**.

---

### **Given:**
- **Arrival rate (λ):** 11.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ):** 11 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s):** 1  
- **Callers wait in line if all agents are busy** (standard M/M/1 behavior)

---

### **Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)**

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{11.6}{11} \approx 1.0545
$$

This is **greater than 1**, which means the system is **unstable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, and the queue will grow indefinitely.

---

### **Conclusion:**

Since the system is **unstable** (ρ > 1), the **long-run average waiting time** in the queue **does not exist** — the queue will grow without bound, and the waiting time will also grow indefinitely.

---

**ANSWER: NONE**

Item 87

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 58.8 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 15 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 60 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 58.8 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 15 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 15 = 60 calls per hour  

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable
The system is stable if the arrival rate is less than the total service rate:

$$
\lambda < c \mu \Rightarrow 58.8 < 60
$$

This condition is satisfied, so the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{58.8}{60} = 0.98
$$

This is the **traffic intensity per server**, and since it is less than 1, the system is stable.

---

### Step 3: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1}) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

This is complex, so we use the **approximation** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q \approx \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### a. Compute the sum of the Poisson probabilities up to c-1:

$$
\sum_{k=0}^{3} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = \frac{0.98^0}{0!} + \frac{0.98^1}{1!} + \frac{0.98^2}{2!} + \frac{0.3844}{6}
$$

$$
= 1 + 0.98 + 0.4802 + 0.16147 = 2.62167
$$

#### b. Compute the numerator:

$$
\rho^c = 0.98^4 = 0.922368
$$

#### c. Compute the denominator:

$$
c(1 - \rho) \cdot \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = 4(1 - 0.98) \cdot 2.62167 = 4(0.02) \cdot 2.62167 = 0.209734
$$

#### d. Compute the waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.922368}{0.209734} \cdot \frac{1}{15} = 4.397 \cdot \frac{1}{15} = 0.2931 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.2931 \times 60 = 17.586 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 17.6

Item 88

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 9.5 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 10 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 9.5 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 10 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

We are asked to find the **long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers**, which is the **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**.

---

### Step 1: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue in an M/M/1 queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

---

### Step 2: Plug in the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{9.5}{10(10 - 9.5)} = \frac{9.5}{10 \times 0.5} = \frac{9.5}{5} = 1.9 \text{ hours}
$$

---

### Step 3: Convert to minutes:

$$
1.9 \text{ hours} = 1.9 \times 60 = 114 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 114.0

Item 89

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 30 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 15 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 30 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 15 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{30}{4 \times 15} = \frac{30}{60} = 0.5
$$

Since ρ < 1, the system is stable and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(1 - \rho) \left( c\mu - \lambda \right)}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \rho = \frac{\lambda}{c\mu} = 0.5 $
- $ \lambda = 30 $, $ \mu = 15 $, $ c = 4 $

First, compute $ P_0 $:

$$
P_0 = \left( \sum_{n=0}^{c} \frac{(\lambda / \mu)^n}{n!} \right)^{-1}
$$

$$
\frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{30}{15} = 2
$$

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{4} \frac{2^n}{n!} = \frac{2^0}{0!} + \frac{2^1}{1!} + \frac{2^2}{2!} + \frac{2^3}{3!} + \frac{2^4}{4!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 2 + 2 + \frac{8}{6} + \frac{16}{24} = 1 + 2 + 2 + 1.333 + 0.667 = 7
$$

$$
P_0 = \frac{1}{7}
$$

Now compute $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(1 - \rho) \left( c\mu - \lambda \right)}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{\frac{1}{7} \cdot 2^4}{4!(1 - 0.5)(60 - 30)} = \frac{\frac{1}{7} \cdot 16}{24 \cdot 0.5 \cdot 30}
$$

$$
= \frac{\frac{16}{7}}{360} = \frac{16}{2520} = \frac{2}{315} \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = \frac{2}{315} \times 60 = \frac{120}{315} \approx 0.381 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.4

Item 90

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 15.7 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 16 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 15.7 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 16 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

We are asked to find the **long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers**, which is the **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**.

---

### Step 1: Use the M/M/1 queue formulas

For an M/M/1 queue:

- **Utilization (ρ)** = λ / μ  
- **Average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** = λ / [μ(μ - λ)]

---

### Step 2: Plug in the values

- λ = 15.7 calls/hour  
- μ = 16 calls/hour  

First, calculate **ρ**:

$$
ρ = \frac{15.7}{16} = 0.98125
$$

Now calculate **W_q**:

$$
W_q = \frac{15.7}{16(16 - 15.7)} = \frac{15.7}{16 \times 0.3} = \frac{15.7}{4.8} \approx 3.2708 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
3.2708 \text{ hours} \times 60 = 196.25 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 196.3

Item 92

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 32 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 10 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 40 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 32 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 10 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 10 = 40 calls per hour  

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable
The system is stable if the arrival rate is less than the total service rate:

$$
\lambda < c \mu \Rightarrow 32 < 40
$$

✅ **The system is stable.**

---

### Step 2: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{32}{40} = 0.8
$$

---

### Step 3: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1}) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

This is complex, so we use the **approximation formula** for **Wq** in an M/M/c queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### a. Compute the sum of the Poisson probabilities up to c-1:

$$
\sum_{k=0}^{3} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = \frac{0.8^0}{0!} + \frac{0.8^1}{1!} + \frac{0.8^2}{2!} + \frac{0.8^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 0.8 + 0.32 + 0.1707 = 2.2907
$$

#### b. Compute the numerator:

$$
\rho^c = 0.8^4 = 0.4096
$$

#### c. Compute the denominator:

$$
c(1 - \rho) \cdot \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = 4(1 - 0.8) \cdot 2.2907 = 4 \cdot 0.2 \cdot 2.2907 = 1.83256
$$

#### d. Compute Wq:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.4096}{1.83256} \cdot \frac{1}{10} = 0.2235 \cdot 0.1 = 0.02235 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.02235 \times 60 = 1.341 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 1.3

Item 93

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 22.4 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 7 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 28 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to find the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 22.4 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 7 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 7 = 28 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (28 calls/hour) is greater than the arrival rate (22.4 calls/hour), the system is **stable**, and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{22.4}{28} = 0.8
$$

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But a more commonly used formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Alternatively, we can use the **Erlang C formula** to compute the probability that all servers are busy, and then use that to compute the average waiting time.

Let’s compute the **Erlang C formula**:

$$
C(c, \lambda, \mu) = \frac{\left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c! \left( 1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}}
$$

But for the **average waiting time in the queue**, we can use the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{\mu} \cdot \frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Plugging in the values:

- $ \rho = 0.8 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ \mu = 7 $
- $ \lambda = 22.4 $

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{0.8^4}{4!(1 - 0.8)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - 0.8}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{0.4096}{24 \cdot 0.2} \cdot \frac{1}{0.2}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{7} \cdot \frac{0.4096}{4.8} \cdot 5
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{1}{7} \cdot 0.085333 \cdot 5 = \frac{1}{7} \cdot 0.426666 = 0.060952 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.060952 \times 60 = 3.657 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 3.7

Item 95

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 7.6 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 8 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 8 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., inter-arrival times are exponentially distributed).
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed**.
- There is **1 agent**.
- The service rate is **8 calls per hour**.
- The arrival rate is **7.6 calls per hour**.

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

For an M/M/1 queue to be stable, the **arrival rate (λ)** must be **less than the service rate (μ)**.

- λ = 7.6 calls/hour
- μ = 8 calls/hour

Since **λ < μ**, the system is **stable** in the long run. Therefore, the staffing level is **workable**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue

The formula for the **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** in an M/M/1 queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{7.6}{8(8 - 7.6)} = \frac{7.6}{8 \times 0.4} = \frac{7.6}{3.2} = 2.375 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
2.375 \times 60 = 142.5 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 142.5

Item 96

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 14.3 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 13 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queueing system** with the following characteristics:

- **Arrival rate (λ)**: 14.3 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)**: 13 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)**: 1  
- **Service time distribution**: Exponential  
- **Arrival process**: Poisson  

This is a **M/M/1 queue** (Markovian arrival, Markovian service, 1 server).

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{14.3}{13} \approx 1.1
$$

Since ρ > 1, the system is **unstable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, and the queue will grow indefinitely. Therefore, the **long-run average waiting time** in the queue **does not exist** in a stable sense.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 98

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 14.7 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 15 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 15 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/1 queue model**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., inter-arrival times are exponentially distributed).
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed**.
- There is **1 agent**.
- The service rate is **15 calls per hour**.
- The arrival rate is **14.7 calls per hour**.

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

For an M/M/1 queue to be stable, the **arrival rate (λ)** must be **less than the service rate (μ)**.

- λ = 14.7 calls/hour
- μ = 15 calls/hour

Since **λ < μ**, the system is **stable** in the long run. Therefore, the staffing level is **workable**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue

The **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** for an M/M/1 queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{14.7}{15(15 - 14.7)} = \frac{14.7}{15 \times 0.3} = \frac{14.7}{4.5} \approx 3.2667 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
W_q = 3.2667 \times 60 \approx 196.0 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 196.0

Model reply

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** with a rate of **λ = 22 calls per hour**.
- Each agent can handle calls at a rate of **μ = 5 calls per hour**.
- There are **s = 4 agents**.
- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are served in **order** (FIFO).
- Call lengths are **exponentially distributed**.

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Multi-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity is defined as:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
\rho = \frac{22}{4 \times 5} = \frac{22}{20} = 1.1
$$

Since **ρ > 1**, the system is **unstable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, and the queue will grow indefinitely.

---

### Step 2: Interpret the result

Because the system is **unstable** (ρ > 1), the **long-run average waiting time** in the queue **does not exist** — the queue will grow without bound, and the waiting time will also grow indefinitely.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 104

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 22.8 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 6 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queue** system with **multiple servers** (agents), where:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** with an average rate of **λ = 22.8 calls per hour**.
- Each agent can handle calls at a rate of **μ = 6 calls per hour**.
- There are **s = 4 agents**.
- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are served in **order** (FIFO).
- Call lengths are **exponentially distributed**.

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Multi-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity per server is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{22.8}{4 \times 6} = \frac{22.8}{24} = 0.95
$$

This means that, on average, the system is **95% busy**, which is **very close to full utilization**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

For an **M/M/s** queue, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{n=0}^{s-1} \frac{\rho^n}{n!} \right) \cdot \mu}
$$

But this is complex to compute manually. Instead, we can use the **approximation** for the average waiting time in the queue for an **M/M/s** queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{1 - \rho} \right)
$$

But this is still not the most accurate. A better approach is to use the **formula for the average waiting time in the queue** in an **M/M/s** queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{n=0}^{s-1} \frac{\rho^n}{n!} \right) \cdot \mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

---

### Step 3: Compute the sum of the series

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{s-1} \frac{\rho^n}{n!} = \sum_{n=0}^{3} \frac{0.95^n}{n!}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.95^0}{0!} + \frac{0.95^1}{1!} + \frac{0.95^2}{2!} + \frac{0.38025}{6}
$$

$$
= 1 + 0.95 + 0.45125 + 0.1584375 = 2.5596875
$$

---

### Step 4: Plug into the formula

$$
W_q = \frac{0.95^4}{4(1 - 0.95) \cdot 2.5596875 \cdot 6}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.81450625}{4 \cdot 0.05 \cdot 2.5596875 \cdot 6}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.81450625}{4 \cdot 0.05 \cdot 15.358125}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.81450625}{3.071625}
$$

$$
\approx 0.265 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
0.265 \times 60 = 15.9 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 15.9

Model reply

- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed** (service rate μ = 25 calls per hour),
- There is **1 agent** (so it's an M/M/1 queue).

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the **arrival rate is less than the service rate**:

$$
\lambda < \mu
$$

$$
24.5 < 25 \quad \text{✓}
$$

So, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue $ W_q $ is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

W_q = \frac{24.5}{25(25 - 24.5)} = \frac{24.5}{25 \times 0.5} = \frac{24.5}{12.5} = 1.96 \text{ hours}

Item 107

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 19.6 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 7 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 28 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to find the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 19.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 7 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 7 = 28 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (28 calls/hour) is greater than the arrival rate (19.6 calls/hour), the system is **stable**, and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{19.6}{28} = 0.7
$$

---

### Step 2: Use the Erlang formula to compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \cdot \left( \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} \right)^c}{c \mu (1 - \rho)}
$$

Where $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system, and can be computed as:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{(\lambda / \mu)^k}{k!} + \frac{(\lambda / \mu)^c}{c! \cdot (1 - \rho)} \right]^{-1}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

---

### Step 3: Compute $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{19.6}{7} = 2.8 $

Now compute the sum:

$$
\sum_{k=0}^{3} \frac{(2.8)^k}{k!} = \frac{(2.8)^0}{0!} + \frac{(2.8)^1}{1!} + \frac{(2.8)^2}{2!} + \frac{(2.8)^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 2.8 + \frac{7.84}{2} + \frac{21.952}{6} = 1 + 2.8 + 3.92 + 3.6587 = 11.3787
$$

Now compute the second term:

$$
\frac{(2.8)^4}{4! \cdot (1 - 0.7)} = \frac{61.4656}{24 \cdot 0.3} = \frac{61.4656}{7.2} \approx 8.5369
$$

Now compute $ P_0 $:

$$
P_0 = \left[ 11.3787 + 8.5369 \right]^{-1} = \left[ 19.9156 \right]^{-1} \approx 0.0502
$$

Now compute $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0502 \cdot (0.7)^4}{28 \cdot (1 - 0.7)} = \frac{0.0502 \cdot 0.2401}{28 \cdot 0.3}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.01205}{8.4} \approx 0.0014345 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.0014345 \times 60 \approx 0.086 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.1

Item 112

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 22.5 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 23 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 22.5 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 23 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{22.5}{23} \approx 0.9783
$$

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.9783}{23(1 - 0.9783)} = \frac{0.9783}{23 \times 0.0217} \approx \frac{0.9783}{0.4991} \approx 1.96 \text{ hours}
$$

---

### Step 3: Convert to minutes

$$
1.96 \text{ hours} \times 60 = 117.6 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 117.6

Item 113

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 19.6 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 20 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **queueing system** with the following characteristics:

- **Arrival rate (λ)**: 19.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)**: 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s)**: 4  
- **Total service rate (s × μ)**: 4 × 5 = 20 calls per hour  

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Poisson arrivals, exponential service times, s servers).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} = \frac{19.6}{20} = 0.98
$$

This is the **traffic intensity per server**, and since it is less than 1, the system is stable.

---

### Step 2: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

For an **M/M/s** queue, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{s-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right) \cdot \mu}
$$

But this is complex to compute manually. Instead, we can use the **approximation** for the average waiting time in the queue in an **M/M/s** queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu - \lambda} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{s \mu} \right)
$$

This is an approximation for the average waiting time in the queue, assuming the system is stable.

Let’s compute it:

$$
W_q = \frac{19.6}{20 - 19.6} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{20} \right) = \frac{19.6}{0.4} \cdot \frac{1}{20} = 49 \cdot \frac{1}{20} = 2.45 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
2.45 \text{ hours} \times 60 = 147 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 147.0

Item 114

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 35.2 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 8 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 32 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we use the **M/M/c queue model**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 35.2 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 8 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 8 = 32 calls per hour  

This is an **M/M/4** queue with:

- **λ = 35.2**  
- **μ = 8**  
- **c = 4**

We are asked to find the **long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers**, which is the **expected waiting time in the queue (W_q)**.

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if **λ < c × μ**:

$$
35.2 < 32 \quad \text{→ Not true}
$$

This means the system is **not stable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate. Therefore, the queue will grow indefinitely, and the **long-run average waiting time** is **undefined** or **infinite**.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 115

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 14 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 7 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **queueing system** with the following characteristics:

- **Arrival rate (λ)**: 14 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)**: 7 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s)**: 4  
- **Callers wait in a single line if all agents are busy** → This is an **M/M/s** queue.

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity is given by:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
\rho = \frac{14}{4 \times 7} = \frac{14}{28} = 0.5
$$

This is less than 1, so the system is stable.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/s** queue, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But this is complex. A simpler and more accurate formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Alternatively, we can use the **approximate formula** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But the more accurate and commonly used formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Let’s compute it step by step.

---

### Step 3: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Substitute the values:

- $ \rho = 0.5 $
- $ s = 4 $
- $ \mu = 7 $
- $ \lambda = 14 $

$$
W_q = \frac{(0.5)^4}{4(1 - 0.5) \cdot 7 \cdot (4 - 0.5)^{4 - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - 0.5}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0625}{4 \cdot 0.5 \cdot 7 \cdot (3.5)^3} \cdot 2
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0625}{4 \cdot 0.5 \cdot 7 \cdot 42.875} \cdot 2
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0625}{4 \cdot 0.5 \cdot 7 \cdot 42.875} \cdot 2
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0625}{600.25} \cdot 2
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.125}{600.25} \approx 0.0002083 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.0002083 \times 60 \approx 0.0125 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

$$
\boxed{\text{ANSWER: 0.0 minutes}}
$$

(Note: This is a very small number, so the average waiting time is effectively zero in practical terms.)

Model reply

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 9.5 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 10 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{9.5}{10} = 0.95
$$

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}

If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 44.9 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 11 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 11 = 44 calls per hour

$$
\lambda < c \mu
$$

Item 120

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 15.3 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 17 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 17 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, since:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** (i.e., inter-arrival times are exponential).
- Call handling times are **exponentially distributed**.
- There is **1 agent** (server).
- Callers who find the agent busy **wait in a single line** and are served in order.

---

### Given:
- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 15.3 calls per hour
- **Service rate (μ)** = 17 calls per hour

---

### Step 1: Compute the utilization factor (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{15.3}{17} \approx 0.90
$$

This means the server is busy about 90% of the time.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/1 queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.90}{17(1 - 0.90)} = \frac{0.90}{17 \times 0.10} = \frac{0.90}{1.7} \approx 0.5294 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
W_q = 0.5294 \times 60 \approx 31.76 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 31.8

Item 124

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 58.8 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 14 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **single-server queue** with **multiple servers** (agents), where:

- Calls arrive according to a **Poisson process** with rate $ \lambda = 58.8 $ calls per hour.
- Each agent can handle calls at a rate of $ \mu = 14 $ calls per hour.
- There are $ s = 4 $ agents.
- Call lengths are **exponentially distributed**.
- Callers who find all agents busy wait in a **single line** and are answered in order.

This is a **M/M/s** queue (Multi-server queue with Poisson arrivals and exponential service times).

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity per server is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{58.8}{4 \times 14} = \frac{58.8}{56} \approx 1.05
$$

Since $ \rho > 1 $, the system is **unstable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, and the queue will grow indefinitely.

---

### Step 2: Interpret the result

Because the system is **unstable** (i.e., the utilization of the servers exceeds 100%), the **long-run average waiting time** in the queue **does not exist** — the queue will grow without bound, and the waiting time will also grow indefinitely.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 125

Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 13 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>

ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 12.7 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 13 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

We are asked to find the **long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers**, which is the **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)**.

---

### Step 1: Use the M/M/1 queue formula for average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{12.7}{13(13 - 12.7)} = \frac{12.7}{13 \times 0.3} = \frac{12.7}{3.9} \approx 3.2564 \text{ hours}
$$

$$

ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using **queueing theory**, specifically the **M/M/c** queue model, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 35.3 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 9 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 9 = 36 calls per hour  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the **arrival rate is less than the total service rate**:

$$
\lambda < c \mu
$$

$$
35.3 < 36 \quad \text{✓}
$$

So, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

We use the **M/M/c queue** formulas. First, compute the **traffic intensity (ρ)**:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{35.3}{36} \approx 0.9806
$$

Now, compute the **average waiting time in the queue (Wq)** using the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right) \cdot \mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### Step 2.1: Compute the sum in the denominator

$$
\sum_{k=0}^{3} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = \frac{\rho^0}{0!} + \frac{\rho^1}{1!} + \frac{\rho^2}{2!} + \frac{\rho^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + \rho + \frac{\rho^2}{2} + \frac{\rho^3}{6}
$$

Substitute $ \rho \approx 0.9806 $:

$$
= 1 + 0.9806 + \frac{(0.9806)^2}{2} + \frac{(0.9806)^3}{6}
$$

$$
\approx 1 + 0.9806 + \frac{0.9616}{2} + \frac{0.9431}{6}
$$

$$
\approx 1 + 0.9806 + 0.4808 + 0.1572 = 2.6186
$$

#### Step 2.2: Compute the numerator

$$
\rho^c = (0.9806)^4 \approx 0.9236
$$

#### Step 2.3: Compute the full expression

$$
W_q = \frac{0.9236}{4(1 - 0.9806) \cdot 2.6186 \cdot 9}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.9236}{4 \cdot 0.0194 \cdot 2.6186 \cdot 9}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.9236}{4 \cdot 0.0194 \cdot 23.5674}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.9236}{1.794}
\approx 0.515 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to **minutes**:

$$
0.515 \times 60 \approx 30.9 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 30.9

Item 128

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 28 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 14 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 56 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to find the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 28 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 14 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
\rho = \frac{28}{4 \times 14} = \frac{28}{56} = 0.5
$$

Since ρ < 1, the system is stable and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(c\mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system
- $ \lambda = 28 $
- $ \mu = 14 $
- $ c = 4 $

First, compute $ \rho = \frac{\lambda}{c\mu} = 0.5 $

Now compute $ P_0 $ using the formula:

$$
P_0 = \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c} \frac{(\lambda / \mu)^k}{k!} \right)^{-1}
$$

$$
\sum_{k=0}^{4} \frac{(28/14)^k}{k!} = \sum_{k=0}^{4} \frac{2^k}{k!}
$$

$$
= \frac{2^0}{0!} + \frac{2^1}{1!} + \frac{2^2}{2!} + \frac{2^3}{3!} + \frac{2^4}{4!}
= 1 + 2 + 2 + \frac{8}{6} + \frac{16}{24}
= 1 + 2 + 2 + 1.333 + 0.667 = 7
$$

So:

$$
P_0 = \frac{1}{7}
$$

Now compute $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(c\mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

$$
= \frac{\frac{1}{7} \cdot 2^4}{4!(56 - 28)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
= \frac{\frac{1}{7} \cdot 16}{24 \cdot 28^2} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
$$

$$
= \frac{16}{7 \cdot 24 \cdot 784} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
= \frac{16}{13728} \cdot \frac{1}{14}
= \frac{16}{192192}
\approx 0.00008326
$$

Convert to hours:

$$
W_q \approx 0.00008326 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q \approx 0.00008326 \times 60 \approx 0.0049956 \text{ minutes}
$$

So the **long-run average waiting time in line is approximately 0.0 minutes**.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.0

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 24 calls per hour. It has 1 agent. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 24 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 24 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 24 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

In an M/M/1 queue, the **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** is given by the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Plugging in the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{24}{24(24 - 24)} = \frac{24}{24 \cdot 0} = \text{undefined}
$$

This indicates that the system is **saturated** (i.e., the arrival rate equals the service rate), and the queue will grow indefinitely. Therefore, the **long-run average waiting time** is **infinite**.

Item 131

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 47 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 12 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Together, the agents can complete 48 calls per hour. Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 47 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 12 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 12 = 48 calls per hour  

Since the total service rate (48) is greater than the arrival rate (47), the system is **stable**, and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 1: Compute traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{47}{48} \approx 0.9792
$$

This is the average utilization of the system.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot (c! \cdot (1 - \rho)^{c - 1}) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

This is complex, so we use the **approximation** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q \approx \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho) \cdot \left( \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} \right)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step.

#### Step 2.1: Compute the sum of the Poisson probabilities up to c-1

$$
\sum_{k=0}^{3} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = \frac{0.9792^0}{0!} + \frac{0.9792^1}{1!} + \frac{0.9792^2}{2!} + \frac{0.3836}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 0.9792 + 0.4796 + 0.1299 = 2.5887
$$

#### Step 2.2: Compute the numerator

$$
\rho^c = 0.9792^4 \approx 0.919
$$

#### Step 2.3: Compute the denominator

$$
c(1 - \rho) \cdot \sum_{k=0}^{c-1} \frac{\rho^k}{k!} = 4(1 - 0.9792) \cdot 2.5887 = 4(0.0208) \cdot 2.5887 \approx 0.2144
$$

#### Step 2.4: Compute the average waiting time in the queue

$$
W_q = \frac{0.919}{0.2144} \cdot \frac{1}{12} \approx 4.286 \cdot \frac{1}{12} \approx 0.357 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.357 \times 60 \approx 21.4 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 21.4

Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as a **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 31.4 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 8 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  
- **Total service rate (c × μ)** = 4 × 8 = 32 calls per hour  

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable
The system is stable if the arrival rate is less than the total service rate:

$$
\lambda < c \mu \Rightarrow 31.4 < 32
$$

This condition is satisfied, so the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu} = \frac{31.4}{32} \approx 0.98125
$$

This is the **traffic intensity per server**, and since it is less than 1, the system is stable.

---

### Step 3: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

For an **M/M/c queue**, the average waiting time in the queue is given by:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^c}{c(1 - \rho)(c \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step:

- $ \rho = 0.98125 $
- $ c = 4 $

- $ c(1 - \rho) = 4(1 - 0.98125) = 4 \times 0.01875 = 0.075 $
- $ c \mu - \lambda = 32 - 31.4 = 0.6 $
- $ \mu = 8 $

Now plug into the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.926}{0.075 \times 0
.6} \cdot \frac{1}{8}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.926}{0.045} \cdot \frac{1}{8} \approx 20.578 \cdot \frac{1}{8} \approx 2.572 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 2.572 \times 60 \approx 154.3 \text{ minutes}
$$

### Final Answer:
