Item 138

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 54 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 15 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **queueing system** with the following characteristics:

- **Arrival rate (λ)**: 54 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)**: 15 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s)**: 4  
- **Callers wait in a single line if all agents are busy** → This is an **M/M/s** queue.

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity is given by:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu}
$$

Substitute the values:

$$
\rho = \frac{54}{4 \times 15} = \frac{54}{60} = 0.9
$$

This means the system is **heavily loaded**, but still stable since ρ < 1.

---

### Step 2: Use the formula for average waiting time in the queue (W_q)

For an **M/M/s** queue, the average waiting time in the queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

But this is complex. A simpler and more accurate approach is to use the **approximation** for the average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Alternatively, we can use the **approximation** for the average waiting time in the queue in an M/M/s queue:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \frac{1 \cdot (s \mu - \lambda)}{s \mu - \lambda}
$$

But the more accurate and commonly used formula is:

$$
W_q = \frac{\rho^s}{s(1 - \rho) \mu (s - \rho)^{s - 1}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

However, for practical purposes, we can use the **approximation**:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu (s \mu - \lambda)} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \rho}
$$

Let’s compute it step by step:

- $ s \mu = 4 \times 15 = 60 $
- $ s \mu - \lambda = 60 - 54 = 6 $
- $ \rho = \frac{54}{60} = 0.9 $

Now compute:

$$
W_q = \frac{54}{4 \times 15 \times 6} = \frac{54}{360} = 0.15 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.15 \times 60 = 9 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### ✅ Final Answer:

ANSWER: 9.0

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as an **M/M/1 queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 27 calls per hour  
- **Service rate (μ)** = 27 calls per hour  
- **Number of servers (s)** = 1  

In an M/M/1 queue, the **average waiting time in the queue (W_q)** is given by the formula:

$$
W_q = \frac{\lambda}{\mu(\mu - \lambda)}
$$

Substituting the values:

$$
W_q = \frac{27}{27(27 - 27)} = \frac{27}{27(0)} = \text{undefined}
$$

This indicates that the system is **saturated** (i.e., the arrival rate equals the service rate), and the queue will grow indefinitely. Therefore, the **long-run average waiting time in line is infinite**.

---

ANSWER: NONE

- **Service rate (μ)** = 19 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (s)** = 1

---

### **Step 1: Check if the system is stable**

$$

Item 145

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 42 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 15 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/c queue model**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 42 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 15 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{42}{4 \times 15} = \frac{42}{60} = 0.7
$$

Since **ρ < 1**, the system is **stable** and the staffing level is **workable in the long run**.

---

### Step 2: Compute the average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue for an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(c\mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{42}{15} = 2.8 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ c\mu = 60 $
- $ \lambda = 42 $

We can use the formula for $ P_0 $ in an M/M/c queue:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{n=0}^{c-1} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^n}{n!} + \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \right]^{-1}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step:

- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = 2.8 $
- $ \rho = 0.7 $

Compute the sum:

$$
\sum_{n=0}^{3} \frac{(2.8)^n}{n!} = \frac{(2.8)^0}{0!} + \frac{(2.8)^1}{1!} + \frac{(2.8)^2}{2!} + \frac{(2.8)^3}{3!}
$$

$$
= 1 + 2.8 + \frac{7.84}{2} + \frac{21.952}{6} = 1 + 2.8 + 3.92 + 3.6587 = 11.3787
$$

Now compute the second term:

$$
\frac{(2.8)^4}{4!(1 - 0.7)} = \frac{61.4656}{24 \times 0.3} = \frac{61.4656}{7.2} \approx 8.5369
$$

Now compute $ P_0 $:

$$
P_0 = \left( 11.3787 + 8.5369 \right)^{-1} = \left( 19.9156 \right)^{-1} \approx 0.0502
$$

Now compute $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0502 \cdot (2.8)^4}{4!(60 - 42)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{15}
$$

$$
= \frac{0.0502 \cdot 61.4656}{24 \cdot (18)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{15}
$$

$$
= \frac{3.085}{24 \cdot 324} \cdot \frac{1}{15}
$$

$$
= \frac{3.085}{7776} \cdot \frac{1}{15} \approx \frac{0.0004}{15} \approx 0.000027 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q \approx 0.000027 \times 60 \approx 0.0016 \text{ minutes}
$$

This is extremely small, which is expected in a well-staffed system.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.0 minutes

(Note: The actual value is approximately 0.0016 minutes, which rounds to 0.0 minutes when rounded to one decimal place.)

Item 146

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 18 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run, we analyze the system using the **M/M/c queue model**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 18 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{18}{4 \times 5} = \frac{18}{20} = 0.9
$$

Since **ρ < 1**, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue (Wq) in an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(c\mu - \lambda)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu}
$$

Where:
- $ P_0 $ is the probability that no customers are in the system.
- $ \frac{\lambda}{\mu} = \frac{18}{5} = 3.6 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ c\mu = 20 $
- $ \lambda = 18 $

We first compute $ P_0 $ using the formula:

$$
P_0 = \left[ \sum_{n=0}^{c} \frac{(\lambda/\mu)^n}{n!} \right]^{-1}
$$

$$
P_0 = \left[ \frac{3.6^0}{0!} + \frac{3.6^1}{1!} + \frac{3.6^2}{2!} + \frac{3.6^3}{3!} + \frac{3.6^4}{4!} \right]^{-1}
$$

$$
P_0 = \left[ 1 + 3.6 + 6.48 + 7.776 + 6.048 \right]^{-1} = \left[ 24.804 \right]^{-1} \approx 0.0403
$$

Now compute $ W_q $:

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0403 \cdot (3.6)^4}{4!(20 - 18)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{5}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{0.0403 \cdot 167.9616}{24 \cdot 4} \cdot \frac{1}{5}
$$

$$
W_q = \frac{6.771}{96} \cdot \frac{1}{5} = \frac{0.0705}{5} = 0.0141 \text{ hours}
$$

Convert to minutes:

$$
W_q = 0.0141 \times 60 \approx 0.846 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 0.8 minutes

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 29.4 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 7 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

What is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To solve this problem, we model the call center as a **queueing system** with the following characteristics:

- **Arrival process**: Poisson with rate $ \lambda = 29.4 $ calls per hour.
- **Service process**: Each agent handles calls with exponential service times, and each agent can complete 7 calls per hour, so the service rate per agent is $ \mu = 7 $ calls per hour.
- **Number of servers (agents)**: $ s = 4 $.
- **Queue discipline**: First-come, first-served (FIFO).
- **System type**: This is a **M/M/s** queue (Poisson arrivals, exponential service times, $ s $ servers).

We are asked to find the **long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers**, in **minutes**.

---

### Step 1: Compute the traffic intensity (ρ)

The traffic intensity per server is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{s \mu} = \frac{29.4}{4 \times 7} = \frac{29.4}{28} \approx 1.05
$$

Since $ \rho > 1 $, the system is **unstable** — the arrival rate exceeds the service rate, and the queue will grow indefinitely.

---

### Step 2: Interpret the result

Because the system is **unstable** (i.e., the utilization is greater than 1), the **long-run average waiting time** in the queue **does not exist** — the queue will grow without bound, and the system will not reach a steady state.

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: NONE

Item 148

Question the model saw

A call center receives calls at random (a Poisson process) at an average rate of 19.6 calls per hour. It has 4 agents. Each agent handles one call at a time and can complete an average of 5 calls per hour; call lengths vary at random (exponentially distributed). Callers who find all agents busy wait in a single line and are answered in order. Assume these rates continue indefinitely.

Is this staffing level workable in the long run? If so, what is the long-run average time a caller waits in line before an agent answers, in minutes?

End your reply with one line in exactly this format:
ANSWER: <minutes, rounded to one decimal place>
If there is no valid numeric answer, write:
ANSWER: NONE

Model reply

To determine whether the staffing level is workable in the long run and to compute the long-run average waiting time in line, we can model this as an **M/M/c queue**, where:

- **Arrival rate (λ)** = 19.6 calls per hour  
- **Service rate per agent (μ)** = 5 calls per hour  
- **Number of agents (c)** = 4  

---

### Step 1: Check if the system is stable

The system is stable if the traffic intensity (ρ) is less than 1. For an M/M/c queue, the traffic intensity is:

$$
\rho = \frac{\lambda}{c \mu}
$$

$$
\rho = \frac{19.6}{4 \times 5} = \frac{19.6}{20} = 0.98
$$

Since **ρ < 1**, the system is **stable** in the long run.

---

### Step 2: Compute the long-run average waiting time in the queue (Wq)

The formula for the average waiting time in the queue (Wq) in an M/M/c queue is:

$$
W_q = \frac{P_0 \left( \frac{\lambda}{\mu} \right)^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

But this is complex. A simpler and more commonly used approximation is:

$$
W_q \approx \frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

However, a more practical and accurate approach is to use the **Erlang C formula** to compute the probability that an arriving customer has to wait, and then use that to compute the average waiting time.

Alternatively, we can use the **approximate formula** for average waiting time in the queue:

$$
W_q \approx \frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} \cdot \frac{1}{\mu(1 - \rho)}
$$

Let’s compute this step by step:

- $ \rho = 0.98 $
- $ c = 4 $
- $ \mu = 5 $
- $ \lambda = 19.6 $

First, compute $ \rho^c $:

$$
\rho^c = 0.98^4 \approx 0.922368
$$

Now compute the denominator:

$$
c!(1 - \rho) = 4! \times (1 - 0.98) = 24 \times 0.02 = 0.48
$$

Now compute the numerator:

$$
\rho^c = 0.922368
$$

Now compute the first part of the formula:

$$
\frac{\rho^c}{c!(1 - \rho)} = \frac{0.922368}{0.48} \approx 1.9216
$$

Now compute the second part:

$$
\frac{1}{\mu(1 - \rho)} = \frac{1}{5 \times 0.02} = \frac{1}{0.1} = 10
$$

Now multiply both parts:

$$
W_q \approx 1.9216 \times 10 = 19.216 \text{ minutes}
$$

---

### Final Answer:

ANSWER: 19.2
